If White blocks the check with c3b1, c3d1, or d2c1, Black will likely just take the piece with the Queen, and the King will still be in check (if the block was at d1 or b1) or will be in check again.

Similarly for c3b1 and c3d1.
If White plays c3b1, and Black plays Qxb1, the King at e1 is still in check.
If White plays c3d1, and Black plays Qxd1, the King at e1 is still in check.
Therefore, blocking the check with a piece that can be captured by the Queen without removing the check is not a viable way to get out of check.
Wait, if White plays d2c1, the Queen is at a1, the Bishop is at c1. The check is blocked.
But if Black plays Qxc1, the Queen is now at c1. The King is at e1.
Is the King in check from a Queen at c1?
Yes, the squares b1, c1, d1, e1 are on the same rank.
So if Black captures the blocking piece, the King is back in check.
This means blocking with d2c1, c3b1, or c3d1 only works if the capture doesn't leave the king in check.
But in all three cases (b1, d1, c1), the resulting square is on the same rank as the king e1.
So the only way to truly escape check is to move the King.

Let's re-verify e1e2.

White pieces: K(e1), B(f1), R(h1), P(a2, c2, f2, g2), B(d2), N(c3), P(g4, d5).

So moving the king is the only option.

The check is blocked!
If Black then plays Qxc1, the Queen is now at c1.
The path from c1 to e1 is ♛(c1) -> ⭘(d1) -> ♔(e1).
The King is still in check.
So blocking with c1 only delays the inevitable unless there's a way to protect c1.
But the only way to get out of check *permanently* (or for the turn) is to move the king or capture the queen.
Wait, if I move the king to e2, I'm no longer on the 1st rank.
The Black Queen at a1 is on the 1st rank.
